To determine the limit of the rational function 

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2},
\]

we follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify the Degrees of the Polynomials**

- The **numerator** is \(3x^2 + 2x + 1\), which is a quadratic polynomial (degree 2).
- The **denominator** is \(5x^2 + 4x + 2\), also a quadratic polynomial (degree 2).

Since both the numerator and denominator have the same degree, we can determine the limit by looking at the leading coefficients.

---

**Step 2: Determine the Leading Coefficients**

- The **leading coefficient of the numerator** is \(3\).
- The **leading coefficient of the denominator** is \(5\).

**Step 3: Compute the Limit**

For a rational function where the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit as \(x\) approaches infinity is the ratio of the leading coefficients:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{3}{5}.
\]

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 2}} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   
   The numerator \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares, which can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x + 2)(x - 2)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel out the common term \( (x - 2) \):
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2
   \]
   *Note:* This simplification is valid for all \( x \neq 2 \).

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Now, substitute \( x = 2 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

Now, what about cos(x) as x approaches 0? Cos(0) is 1, and since cosine is continuous, lim_{x→0} cos(x) = 1. Therefore, 1/cos(x) as x approaches 0 would be 1/1 = 1.

So putting it together: the limit of [sin(x)/x] * [1/cos(x)] as x approaches 0 is equal to (lim sin(x)/x) multiplied by (lim 1/cos(x)) which is 1*1=1. Therefore, the overall limit should be 1.

Alternatively, I can also think about using L'Hospital's Rule since both numerator and denominator approach 0 as x approaches 0. So applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking derivatives of the numerator and the denominator.

The derivative of tan(x) is sec^2(x), and the derivative of x is 1. So applying L'Hospital's Rule, lim_{x→0} [tan(x)/x] = lim_{x→0} [sec^2(x)/1]. Since sec(0)=1, so sec^2(0)=1. Therefore, this also gives us the limit as 1.

The limit of tan(x) divided by x as x approaches 0 can be found using standard limits and properties of trigonometric functions. 

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Express tan(x):**
   \[
   \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}
   \]

2. **Rewrite the original expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan(x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x \cdot \cos(x)}
   \]

3. **Separate into two limits:**
   \[
   \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \right) \times \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos(x)} \right)
   \]

4. **Evaluate each limit:**
   - The first limit is a standard result:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1
     \]
   - For the second part, since cos(0) = 1 and cosine is continuous:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos(x)} = \frac{1}{\cos(0)} = 1
     \]

5. **Multiply the results:**
   \[
   1 \times 1 = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The limit evaluates to 2 because we can rewrite sin(2x) using a trigonometric identity and then apply known limits. Here's the step-by-step solution:

**Solution:**

We need to find:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(2x)}{x}
\]

Using the double-angle identity for sine, we know that:
\[
\sin(2x) = 2\sin(x)\cos(x)
\]

Substitute this into the limit expression:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{2\sin(x)\cos(x)}{x}
\]

Separate the terms:
\[
= 2 \cdot \lim_{{x \to 0}} \left( \frac{\sin(x)}{x} \right) \cdot \lim_{{x \to 0}} \cos(x)
\]

We know that:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1
\]
and
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \cos(x) = \cos(0) = 1
\]

Therefore:
\[
= 2 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 = 2
\]

The given limit can be solved using L’Hospital's Rule multiple times since it results in an indeterminate form each time.

Step-by-step explanation:

2. Apply L'Hospital's Rule once:
   d/dx (x - sin(x)) = 1 - cos(x)
   d/dx (x³) = 3x²
   New limit: limₓ→₀ (1 - cos(x)) / (3x²)

3. Still an indeterminate form, apply L'Hospital's Rule again:
   d/dx (1 - cos(x)) = sin(x)
   d/dx (3x²) = 6x

   d/dx 6x = 6
   New limit: limₓ→₀ cos(x) / 6

L'Hospital's Rule states that if we have a limit of the form lim f(x)/g(x) where both f(x) and g(x) approach either 0 or infinity, then this limit is equal to the limit of f'(x)/g'(x), provided this latter limit exists.

The limit in question is recognized as the definition of the derivative of the function \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \) evaluated at \( x = 1 \). Therefore, this problem falls under the category of **Derivatives**.

Now, let's compute the limit step-by-step:

1. **Identify the Function and Its Derivative:**
   Let \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \).

2. **Apply the Definition of a Derivative:**
   The given limit is:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{f(x) - f(1)}{x - 1}
   \]
   This is exactly \( f'(1) \), the derivative of \( f \) at \( x = 1 \).

3. **Compute the Derivative:**
   Using the chain rule:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (1 + x^3)^{1/3} = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Derivative at \( x = 1 \):**
   \[
   f'(1) = \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
   \]

So, the limit is \( \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}} \).

**Final Answer:**
The value of the limit is \boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules to each term separately.

1. **Differentiate \( 3x^2 \):**
   Using the power rule, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1} \),
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( -5x \):**
   Applying the power rule,
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (-5x) = -5 \times 1 = -5
   \]
   (Note: The derivative of \( x^1 \) is 1.)

3. **Differentiate \( 4 \):**
   The derivative of any constant is zero,
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (4) = 0
   \]

**Combining all the derivatives:**
\[
f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:

\[
\boxed{6x - 5}
\]

1. **Express \( \frac{1}{x} \) in Power Form:**
   
   We can rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) as \( x^{-1} \).

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   
   The power rule states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1} \).
   
   Here, \( n = -1 \), so:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^{-1} = (-1) x^{-2}
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Rewriting \( x^{-2} \) back to fraction form gives us \( \frac{1}{x^2} \).

4. **Final Answer:**
   
   Combining the results, we have:
   \[

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we'll follow these steps:

   F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x
   \]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we use the product rule. The product rule states that the derivative of a product of two functions is the derivative of the first function times the second function plus the first function times the derivative of the second function.

Let:
- \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives:
- \( u'(x) = -\sin x \)
- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

Apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = (-\sin x)(\ln x) + (\cos x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]
Simplify if possible, but in this case, it's already simplified.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we'll use the product rule.

**Product Rule**: If \( u(x) = f(x)g(x) \), then
\[
u'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)
\]

Let:
- \( f(x) = x \) ⇒ \( f'(x) = 1 \)
- \( g(x) = e^x \) ⇒ \( g'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) = (1)e^x + x(e^x) = (1 + x)e^x
\]

**Answer**: 
The derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \) is \(\boxed{(1 + x)e^{x}}\).

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = e^x
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = 2x \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = e^x
   \]

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the identified functions and their derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = 2x \cdot e^x + x^2 \cdot e^x
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression (if possible):**
   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   F'(x) = e^x (2x + x^2)
   \]
   Or, arranging terms:
   \[
   F'(x) = x^2 e^x + 2x e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{F'(x) = x^2 e^x + 2x e^x}

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the **chain rule**.

The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}f(g(x)) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \]

Applying this to \( e^{x^2} \):
1. Let the outer function be \( e^u \) where \( u = x^2 \).
2. The derivative of \( e^u \) with respect to \( u \) is \( e^u \).
3. The inner function \( u = x^2 \) has a derivative of \( 2x \).

Multiplying these together:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2} \]

**Final Answer**
\boxed{2 x e^{x^2}}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule** for differentiation. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative \( F'(x) \) is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Components:**
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Differentiate Each Component:**
   - \( u'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
   - \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (since the derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \))

3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \ln x + 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\ln x + 1}

The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is found by first computing the first derivative, then applying the product rule to differentiate the result.

**Step-by-step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x)):**
   - Let \( u = x^2 \)
   - Then \( F(x) = e^{u} \), so \( F'(x) = e^{u} \cdot u' \)
   - Compute \( u' = 2x \)
   - Therefore, \( F'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2} \)

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x)):**
   - Now differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \)
   - Use the product rule: if \( h(x) = f(g(x)) \cdot k(g(x)) \), then \( h'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \cdot k(g(x)) + f(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \cdot k'(g(x)) \)
   - Here, let:
     - \( f(u) = 2u \) (where \( u = x^2 \))
     - \( g(x) = e^{x^2} \)
     - \( h(x) = f(g(x)) \cdot k(g(x)) = 2x e^{x^2} \)
   - Apply the product rule:
     - Differentiate \( 2x \): derivative is \( 2 \)
     - Multiply by \( e^{x^2} \): gives \( 2 e^{x^2} \)
     - Add the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) times \( 2x \):
       - Derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) is \( 2x e^{x^2} \)
       - Multiply by \( 2x \): gives \( 4x^2 e^{x^2} \)
   - Combine the terms:
     - \( F''(x) = (2)(e^{x^2}) + (4x^2 e^{x^2}) = 2 e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2} \)
   - Factor out \( 2 e^{x^2} \):
     - \( F''(x) = 2 e^{x^2} (1 + 2x^2) \)

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is found using the quotient rule. 

Given:
- Numerator, \( u = x \)
- Denominator, \( v = 1 + x^2 \)

Compute the derivatives:
- \( \frac{du}{dx} = 1 \)
- \( \frac{dv}{dx} = 2x \)

Apply the quotient rule formula:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:
\[
1(1 + x^2) - x(2x) = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

To find the limit of \(\frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2}\) as \(x\) approaches infinity, follow these steps:

### Step-by-Step Solution:

   - The numerator is a quadratic expression with the highest degree term being \(3x^2\).
   - The denominator is also a quadratic expression with the highest degree term being \(5x^2\).

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by the Highest Power of x:**
   
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{3x^2}{x^2} + \frac{2x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{5x^2}{x^2} + \frac{4x}{x^2} + \frac{2}{x^2}}

   \[
   = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit as x Approaches Infinity:**
   
   - As \(x\) approaches infinity, the terms \(\frac{2}{x}\), \(\frac{1}{x^2}\), \(\frac{4}{x}\), and \(\frac{2}{x^2}\) all approach zero.
   
   \[
   = \frac{3 + 0 + 0}{5 + 0 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}

The problem requires evaluating the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}
\]

\[

\]

\[
1 - \cos x = \frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{x^4}{24} + \dots
\]

For small \(x\), higher-order terms can be neglected, so:
\[
1 - \cos x \approx \frac{x^2}{2}
\]

Substituting back into the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\frac{x^2}{2}}{x^2} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}

To find the limit of \(\frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}\) as \(x\) approaches 2, we can simplify the expression before taking the limit.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator and Denominator:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} = \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2}
   \]

2. **Cancel Common Factors:**
   Since \(x \neq 2\), we can cancel the \((x - 2)\) terms:
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)\cancel{(x - 2)}}{\cancel{(x - 2)}} = x + 2
   \]

3. **Take the Limit:**
   Now, evaluate the limit as \(x\) approaches 2:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\(\boxed{4}\)

\[

Let me try to recall the definition of a derivative. The derivative of a function at a point is the limit of the difference quotient as h approaches zero. So for F(x), that would be:

F'(x) = lim(h→0) [F(x + h) - F(x)] / h

Plugging in F(x) = ln x, we get:

F'(x) = lim(h→0) [ln(x + h) - ln(x)] / h

I remember that ln(a) - ln(b) is equal to ln(a/b), so this simplifies to:

lim(h→0) [ln((x + h)/x)] / h
= lim(h→0) [ln(1 + h/x)] / h

Now, I think there's a standard limit here. When h approaches 0, (h/x) is also approaching 0. So maybe we can use the approximation that ln(1 + ε) ≈ ε when ε is close to zero.

So substituting ε = h/x:

lim(h→0) [ln(1 + h/x)] / h ≈ lim(h→0) (h/x) / h
= lim(h→0) 1/x

Which is just 1/x. So that must be the derivative!

Wait, but am I missing something? Is there another way to verify this?

I remember that integrating 1/x gives ln x + C, so differentiation should reverse that. The integral of 1/x from some a to b is ln(b/a). Therefore, if F(x) = ∫(1/t) dt from t=1 to t=x, then by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, F'(x) = 1/x.

Yes, that makes sense. So combining both methods—the limit definition and the inverse relationship with integration—confirms that the derivative of ln x is indeed 1/x.

I think I got it now. The derivative F'(x) is 1 over x.
</think>

The derivative of F(x) = ln x is found using standard calculus rules, resulting in F'(x) = 1/x.

To find the derivative of the polynomial function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules to each term individually.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   - Using the power rule, the derivative of \( x^n \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
   - Here, \( n = 3 \), so:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^{3-1} = 3x^2
     \]

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   - Apply the constant multiple rule: derivative of \( kx^n \) is \( knx^{n-1} \).
   - Here, \( k = 2 \), so:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(2x^2) = 2 \cdot 2x^{2-1} = 4x
     \]

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
     \]

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   - The derivative of any constant is zero because there's no change.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0
     \]

5. **Combine all derivatives:**
   - Adding up all the individual derivatives:
     \[
     f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1
     \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: \{"Final Answer"\} : "3x² + 4x + 1"}
\]

2. Find their derivatives: \( u'(x) = -\sin(x) \) and \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

To find the derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the function:**
   \[
   y = x^x
   \]

2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   Using the logarithmic identity \( \ln(a^b) = b \ln(a) \):
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \ln(x)
   \]

3. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Left side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side (using the product rule on \( x \ln(x) \)):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [x \ln(x)] = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
     
   Putting it together:
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]

4. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
   
5. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x^{x}(\ln(x) + 1)}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we'll use the **product rule** from calculus. 

The product rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( F'(x) \) is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   - Let \( u(x) = x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

2. **Compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   - \( u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)
   - \( v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x \)

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting in the known derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{e^{x} (x+1)}
\]

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let:
\[
u(x) = x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = e^x
\]

Find the derivatives:

\[

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use basic calculus rules, specifically the power rule and the chain rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Function Using a Negative Exponent:**
   
   We can rewrite \( F(x) \) as:
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]
   
2. **Apply the Power Rule and Chain Rule:**
   
   The power rule states that if \( f(u) = u^n \), then \( f'(u) = n \cdot u^{n-1} \cdot u' \).
   
   Here, let \( u = x^2 + 1 \) and \( n = -1 \). Applying the rules:
   \[
   F'(x) = -1 \cdot (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 + 1)
   \]
   
3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( u = x^2 + 1 \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 + 1) = 2x
   \]
   
4. **Combine the Results:**
   
   Substitute back into the derivative:
   \[
   F'(x) = -1 \cdot (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use the chain rule. 

The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), then its derivative is \( f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( e^u \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate each part**:
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \): \( \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u \)
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x \)

3. **Multiply the derivatives together**:
   - \( \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x \)

**Final Answer**:  
The derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x e^{x^2} \).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), I will use the chain rule. The chain rule states that if we have a composite function, its derivative is the derivative of the outer function evaluated at the inner function multiplied by the derivative of the inner function.

In this case, the outer function is the natural logarithm, and the inner function is \( \sin x \).

First, I'll find the derivative of the outer function with respect to the inner function. The derivative of \( \ln(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( \frac{1}{u} \).

Next, I'll find the derivative of the inner function \( \sin x \) with respect to \( x \), which is \( \cos x \).

Finally, I'll multiply these two derivatives together: \( \frac{1}{\sin x} \times \cos x = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} \). This simplifies to \( \cot x \).

Therefore, the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \) is \( F'(x) = \cot x \).
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( h(x) = f(g(x)) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is:

\[
h'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
\]

In our case:
- The **outer function** \( f(u) \) is \( \ln(u) \).
- The **inner function** \( g(x) \) is \( \sin x \).

First, find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):

\[
f'(u) = \frac{1}{u}
\]

Next, evaluate this at the inner function:

\[
f'(\sin x) = \frac{1}{\sin x}
\]

Now, find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):

\[
g'(x) = \cos x
\]

Finally, multiply these derivatives together using the chain rule:

\[
F'(x) = f'(\sin x) \cdot g'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \cot x
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \) is:

**Final Answer**

{'Final Answer': 'cot(x)'}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we can use the quotient rule. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u = e^x \) and \( v = 1 + e^x \).
- Then, \( u' = e^x \) and \( v' = e^x \).

Applying the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^x \cdot e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:

\[
= \frac{e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) \) is \( \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2} \).

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using implicit differentiation. 

Let \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), which implies \( \tan(y) = x \). Differentiating both sides with respect to \(x\):

\[ \frac{d}{dx} [\tan(y)] = \frac{d}{dx} [x] \]
\[ \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)} \]

Since \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), we can express \( \sec^2(y) \) in terms of \(x\). Knowing that \( \tan(y) = x \), the hypotenuse of the right triangle formed is \( \sqrt{1 + x^2} \), so:

\[ \sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2 \]

Therefore, 

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \]

**Final Answer**
The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is \(\boxed{\frac{1}{1 + x^2}}\).

I need to find the limit of the function \(2x^2 - 5x + 1\) as \(x\) approaches 3.

First, I recognize that this is a polynomial function. Polynomials are continuous everywhere on their domain, which includes all real numbers.

Since there are no restrictions or discontinuities at \(x = 3\), the limit can be evaluated by directly substituting \(x = 3\) into the function.

Calculating the value:

\(2(3)^2 - 5(3) + 1 = 2 \times 9 - 15 + 1 = 18 - 15 + 1\)

Simplifying further:

\(18 - 15 = 3\) and \(3 + 1 = 4\).

**Solution:**

We evaluate:
\[

\]

\[

\]

\[

The limit simplifies after factoring the numerator and cancelling the common term (x - 1). Evaluating the simplified expression at x = 1 yields the result.

**Solution:**

We start with:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

Factor the numerator using the difference of cubes formula \(a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2)\):
\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Substitute back into the limit expression:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
\]

Cancel the common factor of \(x - 1\) (since \(x \neq 1\)):
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Evaluate the simplified expression at \(x = 1\):
\[
(1)^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{3}

The problem involves finding the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} \).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Form:**
   The expression \( \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} \) evaluates to \( \frac{0}{0} \) as \( x \to 0 \), which is an indeterminate form.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   Since we have an indeterminate form, apply L'Hospital's Rule by differentiating the numerator and denominator with respect to \( x \).

   - Derivative of numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(1 - \cos x) = \sin x \)
   - Derivative of denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x \)

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   After applying L'Hospital's Rule, the expression becomes:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{2x}
   \]

4. **Evaluate Again:**
   This new limit also evaluates to \( \frac{0}{0} \), so apply L'Hospital's Rule once more.

   - Derivative of numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(\sin x) = \cos x \)
   - Derivative of denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(2x) = 2 \)

5. **Final Simplification:**
   The expression now is:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{2}
   \]

6. **Compute the Limit:**
   As \( x \to 0 \), \( \cos x \to 1 \). Therefore, the limit evaluates to:
   \[
   \frac{1}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The value of the limit is \(\boxed{\dfrac{1}{2}}\).

Alternatively, using L’Hospital’s Rule might also work here since directly substituting x = 0 gives an indeterminate form 0/0. To determine the limit of \(\frac{\tan(x)}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches 0, we can use either a Taylor series expansion or L’Hospital’s Rule.

### Using Taylor Series Expansion:
The Taylor series expansion of \(\tan(x)\) around 0 is:
\[
\tan(x) = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \cdots
\]
Dividing by \(x\) gives:
\[
\frac{\tan(x)}{x} = 1 + \frac{x^2}{3} + \cdots
\]
As \(x \to 0\), the higher-order terms vanish, so:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan(x)}{x} = 1
\]

### Using L’Hospital’s Rule:
Direct substitution gives an indeterminate form \(\frac{0}{0}\). Applying L’Hospital’s Rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan(x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2(x)}{1}
\]
Since \(\sec(0) = 1\), we have:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sec^2(x)}{1} = 1
\]

Both methods confirm that the limit is 1.

To find the limit of \(\frac{\sin(2x)}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches 0:

We can use the standard limit \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1\).

Rewriting the given function:
\[
\frac{\sin(2x)}{x} = 2 \cdot \frac{\sin(2x)}{2x}
\]

Now, as \(x \to 0\), let \(\theta = 2x\) which also approaches 0. Thus:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} 2 \cdot \frac{\sin(2x)}{2x} = 2 \cdot \lim_{\theta \to 0} \frac{\sin(\theta)}{\theta} = 2 \times 1 = 2
\]

\[ \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} \]
provided the limit on the right exists.

So, applying L'Hôpital's Rule to \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \):

   - \( f(x) = 2^x \Rightarrow f'(x) = 2^x \ln(2) \)

   \[ \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2^x \ln(2)}{3x^2} \]

This is still of the form \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), so we apply L'Hôpital's Rule again.

2. Second derivative:
   - \( f'(x) = 2^x \ln(2) \Rightarrow f''(x) = 2^x (\ln(2))^2 \)
   - \( g'(x) = 3x^2 \Rightarrow g''(x) = 6x \)

   \[ \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2^x (\ln(2))^3}{6} \]

To find the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \), we can use the basic rule of differentiation for exponential functions. 

**Solution:**

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x
\]

This is because the derivative of \( e^u \) with respect to \( u \) is \( e^u \), and if \( u = x \), then \( \frac{du}{dx} = 1 \). Applying the chain rule:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
\]

**Final Answer:**

The product rule states that if you have two functions multiplied together, their derivative is the derivative of the first function times the second function plus the first function times the derivative of the second function.

Next, find the derivatives of these functions:

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative \( F'(x) \) is given by:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's define:
- \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = -\sin x
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot \ln x + \cos x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
\]

Simplify the expression:
\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\sin(x)\ln(x) + \dfrac{\cos(x)}{x}}

Now, to find dy/dx, I'll differentiate both sides with respect to x. On the left side, using the chain rule, it's (1/y) * dy/dx. On the right side, I need to apply the product rule because it's x multiplied by ln(x). The derivative of x is 1 and the derivative of ln(x) is 1/x.

Putting that together: (1/y) * dy/dx = 1 * ln(x) + x * (1/x). Simplifying the right side, I get ln(x) + 1 because x*(1/x) cancels out to 1.

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. Take the natural logarithm of both sides:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
3. Apply logarithm properties to simplify:
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
4. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side using the chain rule: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \)
   - Right side using the product rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x \cdot \ln(x)) = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
5. Set the derivatives equal and solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \\
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
6. Substitute \( y = x^x \) back into the equation:
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

The problem involves finding the derivative of the product of two functions: \( \sin(x) \) and \( \ln(x) \). 

To solve this, we apply the **product rule**, which states that for two differentiable functions \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), the derivative of their product is given by:

\frac{d}{dx}[u(x)v(x)] = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)

- \( u(x) = \sin(x) \)
- \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by differentiating \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \). This is a product of two functions, so we'll use the **product rule**, which states:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u v] = u' v + u v'
\]
Let:
\[
u = x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v = e^x
\]
Then:
\[
u' = 2x \quad \text{and} \quad v' = e^x
\]
Applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u' v + u v' = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Now, we'll differentiate \( F'(x) \) to find the second derivative. Again, we have a sum of terms involving products of functions.

Differentiate each term in \( F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \):

1. For \( 2x e^x \):
   - Let \( u = 2x \) and \( v = e^x \)
   - Then \( u' = 2 \) and \( v' = e^x \)
   - Applying the product rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) = 2 e^x + 2x e^x
     \]

2. For \( x^2 e^x \):
   - Let \( u = x^2 \) and \( v = e^x \)
   - Then \( u' = 2x \) and \( v' = e^x \)
   - Applying the product rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
     \]

Combine these results to get the second derivative:
\[
F''(x) = (2 e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x) = 2 e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Final Answer**

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:
\[
F''(x) = (2 + 4x + x^2) e^x
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use either the quotient rule or the chain rule.

**Using the Quotient Rule:**
- Let numerator be \( u = 1 \) and denominator be \( v = x^2 + 1 \).
- The derivative is given by:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2} = \frac{(x^2 + 1)(0) - (1)(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

**Using the Chain Rule:**
- Rewrite \( F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \).
- Let \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \), so \( F(x) = g^{-1}(x) \).
- The derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = -1 \cdot (g(x))^{-2} \cdot g'(x) = -1 \cdot (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Both methods yield the same result.

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we will use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), then its derivative is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} f(g(x)) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u = x^2 \) (inner function)
- Then, \( e^{u} = e^{x^2} \) (outer function)

**Step 1:** Find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):
\[
\frac{d}{du} e^{u} = e^{u}

**Step 2:** Find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
\]

**Step 3:** Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives from Step 1 and Step 2:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{u} \cdot 2x = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}

   - \( v' = 2x \)

   \[

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we start by computing the first derivative using the chain rule:

1. **First Derivative**:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left(e^{x^2}\right) = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

2. **Second Derivative**:
   To find \( F''(x) \), we differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \) using the product rule:

   - Let \( f(x) = 2x \) and \( g(x) = e^{x^2} \).
   - Then, \( f'(x) = 2 \) and \( g'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \).

   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   F''(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) = 2e^{x^2} + (2x)(2x e^{x^2}) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
   \]

   Simplifying:
   \[
   F''(x) = (4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer**:  
The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is \( F''(x) = (4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2} \).
